========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, hi, nice to meet you too, Doc. I guess, well, to answer your question... it's kind of hard to explain, but I’ll try. You see, I've been working as a building cleaner—janitorial stuff mostly, over at the corporate park for a good number of years now—and usually that's great for me because I'm always on my feet, you know? Moving around, emptying bins, buffing the floors, always doing something physical. 

But the problem is, whenever I have to actually stop and sit down... I just can't do it. What brings me in is I feel like I’ve got this, this actual motor humming right under my skin whenever I’m supposed to just sit still. It's maddening, really. Like, we have these mandatory staff meetings with the facility managers, and everyone else is just sitting there with their coffee, listening to the new chemical safety protocols or whatever, and I'm practically vibrating. 

Or even on Sundays, during the church event briefings. I help set up the fellowship hall for the potlucks and the youth group nights, which I really enjoy doing, but when the committee actually sits down at the table to plan it all out... oh boy. I end up shifting in my chair, crossing and uncrossing my legs, tapping my boots, and eventually, I just have to stand up. I'll find some excuse, any excuse, really, to get out of that chair. I'll say I need to check the thermostat, or I'll go get a glass of water, or I'll just stand by the back door pretending to check the hinges. I just have to move around. And it's getting to the point where people are looking at me funny, like I'm being disrespectful or impatient, but I just... I can't just sit there. That humming feeling just takes over completely.

Well, I mean, it's definitely there when I'm sitting down, like I was saying about the staff meetings and the church planning committees. That's when it's the most obvious, I guess, because everyone else is just so still, like statues almost, and I'm the only one fidgeting and trying to find an excuse to stand up. But it's not just when I'm sitting still. It's really just this constant thing. 

Like, even when I'm lying down in bed at night, trying to go to sleep. My wife, she's a very light sleeper, bless her heart, and she'll tell you. I'm tossing and turning, flipping the pillow over to the cool side, kicking the blankets off because I feel too restricted, and then pulling them back up because it's drafty. It's like my legs just want to keep walking even though my head knows it's time to sleep. And it's not just my legs, really, it's my whole body. It's like that engine I mentioned, it just doesn't have an off switch. 

Even when I'm doing my job, pushing the buffer or taking out the trash, I feel it. It's just that when I'm moving, the humming kind of blends in with the work, you know? It's like I'm giving the engine somewhere to go. But the second I stop—whether it's sitting in a chair, or waiting in line at the grocery store, or lying in bed—that's when the humming gets so loud, so to speak, that I can't ignore it. It's just always there, humming away, waiting for me to stop moving so it can take over again. It's exhausting, honestly. I just want to be able to sit in a chair or lay in bed and just... be still. But I can't.

It's just that when I'm moving, the humming kind of blends in with the work, you know? It's like I'm giving the engine somewhere to go. It's just always there, humming away, waiting for me to stop moving so it can take over again. It's exhausting, honestly. I just want to be able to sit in a chair or lay in bed and just... be still. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I mean... does moving around make it better? I guess... it doesn't make it go away, if that's what you mean. Like, it's not like a switch flips and the engine turns off just because I stood up or started walking. The humming is still there. But... it's more like, when I'm moving, I'm doing what the humming wants me to do, you know? 

Like, if I'm at the church potluck meeting, and I finally just give up and stand up to go pretend to check the thermostat like I told you... the physical act of walking across the fellowship hall feels like a relief, kind of. Because I'm not fighting the urge anymore. When I'm sitting there, my whole brain is just screaming "move, move, move," and it takes so much energy just to keep my rear end in the chair. So when I finally get up, that pressure kind of releases. 

But the humming itself? The actual feeling under my skin? No, that doesn't really stop. It just gets put to use, I guess. Like when I'm buffing the floors at the corporate park. I'm moving, I'm sweating, I'm wrestling with that machine, and the humming is just part of the momentum. It doesn't feel like a relief when I'm working, it just feels like... like that's my normal state. But the minute I stop buffing and lean against the wall to catch my breath? Boom. There it is again, loud and clear, making me want to start the machine right back up. So, moving doesn't really cure it or make it better, it just... it just makes it so I don't feel like I'm going to explode out of my own skin from trying to hold it all in. I don't know if that makes sense, but it's just... it never really leaves me alone, whether I'm moving or not.
=====================================

But I can't.\n\nDoctor: When you get that humming feeling, does moving around make it feel better?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... does moving around make it better? I guess... it doesn't make it go away, if that's what you mean. Like, it's not like a switch flips and the engine turns off just because I stood up or started walking. The humming is still there. But... it's more like, when I'm moving, I'm doing what the humming wants me to do, you know? \n\nLike, if I'm at the church potluck meeting, and I finally just give up and stand up to go pretend to check the thermostat like I told you... the physical act of walking across the fellowship hall feels like a relief, kind of. Because I'm not fighting the urge anymore. When I'm sitting there, my whole brain is just screaming \"move, move, move,\" and it takes so much energy just to keep my rear end in the chair. So when I finally get up, that pressure kind of releases. \n\nBut the humming itself? The actual feeling under my skin? No, that doesn't really stop. It just gets put to use, I guess. Like when I'm buffing the floors at the corporate park. I'm moving, I'm sweating, I'm wrestling with that machine, and the humming is just part of the momentum. It doesn't feel like a relief when I'm working, it just feels like... like that's my normal state. But the minute I stop buffing and lean against the wall to catch my breath? Boom. There it is again, loud and clear, making me want to start the machine right back up. So, moving doesn't really cure it or make it better, it just... it just makes it so I don't feel like I'm going to explode out of my own skin from trying to hold it all in. I don't know if that makes sense, but it's just... it never really leaves me alone, whether I'm moving or not.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

Well, no, not specifically in my legs. I know I mentioned kicking the blankets off and it feeling like my legs want to keep walking, but it's not like... it's not an ache or a cramp or some weird tingling just in my calves or anything like that. I've had guys I work with talk about that, you know, getting charley horses or this creepy-crawly feeling in their shins after being on their feet all day, especially the older guys who've been doing the heavy lifting and floor stripping for twenty-plus years. 

But for me, it's not like it's isolated to my legs. It's more of a whole-body thing. It's that engine feeling I was talking about. It's in my chest, it's in my arms, it's in my head... it's just this overall restlessness. Like my entire body is just humming with this excess energy that doesn't know where to go. So, sure, my legs feel it, and they're usually the first things to start moving when I'm trying to sit still, like bouncing my knee or tapping my foot, but it's not a localized discomfort in my legs. It's just... I'm restless all over. My wife will tell you, it's not just my legs moving in bed, I'm shifting my shoulders, adjusting my arms, rolling my whole torso over. It's just my whole self that can't settle down.

Moving around, emptying bins, buffing the floors, always doing something physical. \n\nBut the problem is, whenever I have to actually stop and sit down... I just can't do it. What brings me in is I feel like I’ve got this, this actual motor humming right under my skin whenever I’m supposed to just sit still. It's maddening, really. Like, we have these mandatory staff meetings with the facility managers, and everyone else is just sitting there with their coffee, listening to the new chemical safety protocols or whatever, and I'm practically vibrating. \n\nOr even on Sundays, during the church event briefings. I help set up the fellowship hall for the potlucks and the youth group nights, which I really enjoy doing, but when the committee actually sits down at the table to plan it all out... oh boy. I end up shifting in my chair, crossing and uncrossing my legs, tapping my boots, and eventually, I just have to stand up. I'll find some excuse, any excuse, really, to get out of that chair. I'll say I need to check the thermostat, or I'll go get a glass of water, or I'll just stand by the back door pretending to check the hinges. I just have to move around. And it's getting to the point where people are looking at me funny, like I'm being disrespectful or impatient, but I just... I can't just sit there. That humming feeling just takes over completely.\n\nDoctor: Did this restless, unable-to-sit-still feeling start after you began a new medication or changed the dose of one?\n\nPatient: No, no, I don't think so. I mean, I really don't take much to begin with. I've got, you know, just my regular stuff... a little something for my blood pressure that my regular doctor put me on maybe four or five years ago, and I haven't changed the dose on that at all. And I take regular old ibuprofen for my knees, because like I said, pushing those industrial floor buffers—man, you wouldn't believe the torque on those things, they really just pull your whole body around if you aren't bracing yourself right, and at 56, your joints definitely feel it by the end of a long shift. \n\nBut anyway, no, no new prescriptions or anything like that. My wife is actually the one who keeps track of the medicine cabinet anyway, she bought one of those little plastic organizer things with the days of the week printed on the lids, the ones that snap shut? Because honestly, if it was up to me I'd probably forget if I took my morning vitamin or not. But she hasn't put anything new in there, and she hasn't changed how many pills are in the little Tuesday slot or whatever. So it definitely isn't from a pill. It's not a sudden thing like that at all, it's just... it's just how I feel, you know? Just this constant running engine.\n\nDoctor: When you get that humming feeling, is it mainly when you’re sitting still or lying down?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean, it's definitely there when I'm sitting down, like I was saying about the staff meetings and the church planning committees. That's when it's the most obvious, I guess, because everyone else is just so still, like statues almost, and I'm the only one fidgeting and trying to find an excuse to stand up. But it's not just when I'm sitting still. It's really just this constant thing. \n\nLike, even when I'm lying down in bed at night, trying to go to sleep. My wife, she's a very light sleeper, bless her heart, and she'll tell you. I'm tossing and turning, flipping the pillow over to the cool side, kicking the blankets off because I feel too restricted, and then pulling them back up because it's drafty. It's like my legs just want to keep walking even though my head knows it's time to sleep. And it's not just my legs, really, it's my whole body. It's like that engine I mentioned, it just doesn't have an off switch. \n\nEven when I'm doing my job, pushing the buffer or taking out the trash, I feel it. It's just that when I'm moving, the humming kind of blends in with the work, you know? It's like I'm giving the engine somewhere to go. But the second I stop—whether it's sitting in a chair, or waiting in line at the grocery store, or lying in bed—that's when the humming gets so loud, so to speak, that I can't ignore it. It's just always there, humming away, waiting for me to stop moving so it can take over again. It's exhausting, honestly. I just want to be able to sit in a chair or lay in bed and just... be still. But I can't.\n\nDoctor: When you get that humming feeling, does moving around make it feel better?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... does moving around make it better? I guess... it doesn't make it go away, if that's what you mean. Like, it's not like a switch flips and the engine turns off just because I stood up or started walking. The humming is still there. But... it's more like, when I'm moving, I'm doing what the humming wants me to do, you know? \n\nLike, if I'm at the church potluck meeting, and I finally just give up and stand up to go pretend to check the thermostat like I told you... the physical act of walking across the fellowship hall feels like a relief, kind of. Because I'm not fighting the urge anymore. When I'm sitting there, my whole brain is just screaming \"move, move, move,\" and it takes so much energy just to keep my rear end in the chair. So when I finally get up, that pressure kind of releases. \n\nBut the humming itself? The actual feeling under my skin? No, that doesn't really stop. It just gets put to use, I guess. Like when I'm buffing the floors at the corporate park. I'm moving, I'm sweating, I'm wrestling with that machine, and the humming is just part of the momentum. It doesn't feel like a relief when I'm working, it just feels like... like that's my normal state. But the minute I stop buffing and lean against the wall to catch my breath? Boom. There it is again, loud and clear, making me want to start the machine right back up. So, moving doesn't really cure it or make it better, it just... it just makes it so I don't feel like I'm going to explode out of my own skin from trying to hold it all in. I don't know if that makes sense, but it's just... it never really leaves me alone, whether I'm moving or not.\n\nDoctor: When you’re trying to rest, do you feel an uncomfortable sensation specifically in your legs?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not specifically in my legs. I know I mentioned kicking the blankets off and it feeling like my legs want to keep walking, but it's not like... it's not an ache or a cramp or some weird tingling just in my calves or anything like that. I've had guys I work with talk about that, you know, getting charley horses or this creepy-crawly feeling in their shins after being on their feet all day, especially the older guys who've been doing the heavy lifting and floor stripping for twenty-plus years. \n\nBut for me, it's not like it's isolated to my legs. It's more of a whole-body thing. It's that engine feeling I was talking about. It's in my chest, it's in my arms, it's in my head... it's just this overall restlessness. Like my entire body is just humming with this excess energy that doesn't know where to go. So, sure, my legs feel it, and they're usually the first things to start moving when I'm trying to sit still, like bouncing my knee or tapping my foot, but it's not a localized discomfort in my legs. It's just... I'm restless all over. My wife will tell you, it's not just my legs moving in bed, I'm shifting my shoulders, adjusting my arms, rolling my whole torso over. It's just my whole self that can't settle down.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

Remaining differential includes anxiety-related psychomotor agitation, ADHD, and bipolar spectrum.","is_final":false}

It doesn't feel like a relief when I'm working, it just feels like... like that's my normal state. But the minute I stop buffing and lean against the wall to catch my breath? Boom. There it is again, loud and clear, making me want to start the machine right back up. So, moving doesn't really cure it or make it better, it just... it just makes it so I don't feel like I'm going to explode out of my own skin from trying to hold it all in. I don't know if that makes sense, but it's just... it never really leaves me alone, whether I'm moving or not.\n\nDoctor: When you’re trying to rest, do you feel an uncomfortable sensation specifically in your legs?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not specifically in my legs. I know I mentioned kicking the blankets off and it feeling like my legs want to keep walking, but it's not like... it's not an ache or a cramp or some weird tingling just in my calves or anything like that. I've had guys I work with talk about that, you know, getting charley horses or this creepy-crawly feeling in their shins after being on their feet all day, especially the older guys who've been doing the heavy lifting and floor stripping for twenty-plus years. \n\nBut for me, it's not like it's isolated to my legs. It's more of a whole-body thing. It's that engine feeling I was talking about. It's in my chest, it's in my arms, it's in my head... it's just this overall restlessness. Like my entire body is just humming with this excess energy that doesn't know where to go. So, sure, my legs feel it, and they're usually the first things to start moving when I'm trying to sit still, like bouncing my knee or tapping my foot, but it's not a localized discomfort in my legs. It's just... I'm restless all over. My wife will tell you, it's not just my legs moving in bed, I'm shifting my shoulders, adjusting my arms, rolling my whole torso over. It's just my whole self that can't settle down.\n\n[Last Question]\nDid you have this same kind of can’t-sit-still restlessness as a child?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 56 / janitor_or_building_cleaner / associates\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Additional Requirement 1\nmanifestation: \"I remember getting in trouble back in grade school for popping out of my seat, talking over the teacher, and rushing ahead before directions were finished; my mother used to say I acted like I was wound too tight even as a kid.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

  {
    "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, gosh, yeah. Yeah, absolutely. I mean, it's not a new thing, this engine feeling. It's... well, it's been there as long as I can remember, really. 

Like, I think back to when I was just a little guy, back in grade school. I was always the one getting in trouble. I mean, always. My teachers, bless them, they didn't know what to do with me. I was the kid who was always popping out of his seat. I just couldn't stay put! The teacher would be up at the chalkboard, trying to explain long division or spelling words, and I'd just... I'd be out of my chair, walking over to the pencil sharpener, or going to look out the window, or just standing up by my desk because sitting felt like torture. 

And it wasn't just the sitting, either. I was always talking over the teacher, blurting out the answers before she even finished asking the question. Or she'd be handing out a worksheet and trying to give us the directions, and I'd already be halfway done with the first page, just rushing ahead, not really reading anything, just trying to get it done because I couldn't stand the waiting part. I spent a lot of time in the hallway, let me tell you. A lot of time sitting outside the principal's office. 

My mother, she used to say I was like a wind-up toy that got cranked one too many times. "Wound too tight," she'd say. "You're wound too tight, boy." She used to joke that she needed to put a brick on my head just to keep me on the ground. She was a saint, my mom, but I know I exhausted her. So, yeah, this whole... this whole restlessness thing, it's not something that just started when I got older or started working as a janitor. It's just... it's who I've always been, I guess. Just wound too tight, since day one.

What brings me in is I feel like I’ve got this, this actual motor humming right under my skin whenever I’m supposed to just sit still. It's maddening, really. My wife is actually the one who keeps track of the medicine cabinet anyway, she bought one of those little plastic organizer things with the days of the week printed on the lids, the ones that snap shut? But it's not just when I'm sitting still. It's really just this constant thing. \n\nLike, even when I'm lying down in bed at night, trying to go to sleep. I'm tossing and turning, flipping the pillow over to the cool side, kicking the blankets off because I feel too restricted, and then pulling them back up because it's drafty. It's like my legs just want to keep walking even though my head knows it's time to sleep. And it's not just my legs, really, it's my whole body. It's like that engine I mentioned, it just doesn't have an off switch. \n\nEven when I'm doing my job, pushing the buffer or taking out the trash, I feel it. It's just that when I'm moving, the humming kind of blends in with the work, you know? It's like I'm giving the engine somewhere to go. It's just always there, humming away, waiting for me to stop moving so it can take over again. It's exhausting, honestly. I just want to be able to sit in a chair or lay in bed and just... be still. (Note: In adolescents or adults, may be limited to feeling restless.)\n  manifestation: What brings me in is I feel like I’ve got a motor humming under my skin whenever I’m supposed to sit still, like in staff meetings or church event briefings, and I end up shifting, standing, or finding some excuse to move around.\n- key: S013\n  name: Quiet_Activity_Difficulty\n  description: Often unable to play or engage in leisure activities quietly.\n  manifestation: Even when I’m trying to relax, like fishing with my wife or sitting through bingo, I catch myself tapping, talking too loud, messing with my tackle box or pencil, and it gets hard for me to just be quiet and enjoy it.\n- key: S014\n  name: Constant_Activity\n  description: Is often 'on the go', acting as if 'driven by a motor'.\n  manifestation: On my shifts I go from mopping to checking trash to straightening chairs without really stopping, and even at home I’ll start puttering in the garage or re-sorting tools instead of sitting down after work.\n- key: S016\n  name: Blurting_Answers\n  description: Often blurts out an answer before a question has been completed.\n  manifestation: When my supervisor or one of the church staff starts giving me instructions, I jump in with what I think they’re going to say before they finish, and sometimes I get it wrong and have to be corrected.\n- key: S017\n  name: Waiting_Turn_Difficulty\n  description: Often has difficulty waiting his or her turn.\n  manifestation: Waiting for the freight elevator, the supply closet key, or somebody else to decide what room needs cleaning next makes me antsy and short, and I’ll pace or push to get moving before it’s really my turn.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: Onset prior to age 12 years\n  manifestation: I remember getting in trouble back in grade school for popping out of my seat, talking over the teacher, and rushing ahead before directions were finished; my mother used to say I acted like I was wound too tight even as a kid.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: At work it has started causing complaints and corrections because I miss parts of instructions and create friction during room turnovers and supply decisions, to the point my supervisor pushed me toward the employee assistance program.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 5 months\n  manifestation: This worse stretch has been going on for about a year and five months, ever since my cleaning contract changed to rotating hours and more event work.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 56 / janitor_or_building_cleaner / associates\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S012\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: When you get that humming feeling, does moving around make it feel better?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... does moving around make it better? I guess... it doesn't make it go away, if that's what you mean. Like, it's not like a switch flips and the engine turns off just because I stood up or started walking. The humming is still there. But... it's more like, when I'm moving, I'm doing what the humming wants me to do, you know? \n\nLike, if I'm at the church potluck meeting, and I finally just give up and stand up to go pretend to check the thermostat like I told you... the physical act of walking across the fellowship hall feels like a relief, kind of. Because I'm not fighting the urge anymore. When I'm sitting there, my whole brain is just screaming \"move, move, move,\" and it takes so much energy just to keep my rear end in the chair. So when I finally get up, that pressure kind of releases. \n\nBut the humming itself? The actual feeling under my skin? No, that doesn't really stop. It just gets put to use, I guess. Like when I'm buffing the floors at the corporate park. I'm moving, I'm sweating, I'm wrestling with that machine, and the humming is just part of the momentum. It doesn't feel like a relief when I'm working, it just feels like... like that's my normal state. But the minute I stop buffing and lean against the wall to catch my breath? Boom. There it is again, loud and clear, making me want to start the machine right back up. So, moving doesn't really cure it or make it better, it just... it just makes it so I don't feel like I'm going to explode out of my own skin from trying to hold it all in. I don't know if that makes sense, but it's just... it never really leaves me alone, whether I'm moving or not.\n\nDoctor: When you’re trying to rest, do you feel an uncomfortable sensation specifically in your legs?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not specifically in my legs. I know I mentioned kicking the blankets off and it feeling like my legs want to keep walking, but it's not like... it's not an ache or a cramp or some weird tingling just in my calves or anything like that. I've had guys I work with talk about that, you know, getting charley horses or this creepy-crawly feeling in their shins after being on their feet all day, especially the older guys who've been doing the heavy lifting and floor stripping for twenty-plus years. \n\nBut for me, it's not like it's isolated to my legs. It's more of a whole-body thing. It's that engine feeling I was talking about. It's in my chest, it's in my arms, it's in my head... it's just this overall restlessness. Like my entire body is just humming with this excess energy that doesn't know where to go. So, sure, my legs feel it, and they're usually the first things to start moving when I'm trying to sit still, like bouncing my knee or tapping my foot, but it's not a localized discomfort in my legs. It's just... I'm restless all over. My wife will tell you, it's not just my legs moving in bed, I'm shifting my shoulders, adjusting my arms, rolling my whole torso over. It's just my whole self that can't settle down.\n\nDoctor: Did you have this same kind of can’t-sit-still restlessness as a child?\n\nPatient: Oh, gosh, yeah. Yeah, absolutely. I mean, it's not a new thing, this engine feeling. It's... well, it's been there as long as I can remember, really. \n\nLike, I think back to when I was just a little guy, back in grade school. I was always the one getting in trouble. I mean, always. My teachers, bless them, they didn't know what to do with me. I was the kid who was always popping out of his seat. I just couldn't stay put! The teacher would be up at the chalkboard, trying to explain long division or spelling words, and I'd just... I'd be out of my chair, walking over to the pencil sharpener, or going to look out the window, or just standing up by my desk because sitting felt like torture. \n\nAnd it wasn't just the sitting, either. I was always talking over the teacher, blurting out the answers before she even finished asking the question. Or she'd be handing out a worksheet and trying to give us the directions, and I'd already be halfway done with the first page, just rushing ahead, not really reading anything, just trying to get it done because I couldn't stand the waiting part. I spent a lot of time in the hallway, let me tell you. A lot of time sitting outside the principal's office. \n\nMy mother, she used to say I was like a wind-up toy that got cranked one too many times. \"Wound too tight,\" she'd say. \"You're wound too tight, boy.\" She used to joke that she needed to put a brick on my head just to keep me on the ground. She was a saint, my mom, but I know I exhausted her. So, yeah, this whole... this whole restlessness thing, it's not something that just started when I got older or started working as a janitor. It's just... it's who I've always been, I guess. Just wound too tight, since day one.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever had a period where your mood was unusually high, excited, or irritable for days at a time?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, no, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I'm a pretty upbeat guy generally, you know? Like, when I'm working the night shift at the corporate park, I'll have my headphones on, listening to some classic rock or maybe a baseball game if it's the season, and I'm humming along, getting the floors done. I'm energized, sure, because of that engine feeling we talked about, but it's not like an unusual high or anything. It's just my normal, everyday level of... well, of being wound up. 

And irritable? I mean, sure, I get annoyed if the buffing machine breaks down or if somebody tracks mud right over the floor I just stripped and waxed. Who wouldn't get a little ticked off about that, right? You put in two hours of back-breaking work and some guy from accounting just waltzes right through it. But it's not like I'm walking around angry for days on end for no reason. It's just normal frustration. 

I don't have these big swings where I'm suddenly on top of the world or ready to bite everyone's head off for a week straight. It's more of a constant, steady stream of just... going. Always going. My wife sometimes says I can be a bit much when I'm trying to get a project done around the house on a Saturday, like if I decide I'm going to clean out the garage and I'm just tearing through boxes and talking a mile a minute, but that's just because I want to get it done and I've got the momentum going. It's not like my mood itself is somehow out of whack or anything. It's just the energy, you know? Just the engine always running. So, no, nothing like what you're describing. Just the usual me.

Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event. Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nWell, I mean, it's definitely there when I'm sitting down, like I was saying about the staff meetings and the church planning committees. That's when it's the most obvious, I guess, because everyone else is just so still, like statues almost, and I'm the only one fidgeting and trying to find an excuse to stand up. But it's not just when I'm sitting still. It's really just this constant thing. \n\nLike, even when I'm lying down in bed at night, trying to go to sleep. My wife, she's a very light sleeper, bless her heart, and she'll tell you. I'm tossing and turning, flipping the pillow over to the cool side, kicking the blankets off because I feel too restricted, and then pulling them back up because it's drafty. It's like my legs just want to keep walking even though my head knows it's time to sleep. And it's not just my legs, really, it's my whole body. It's like that engine I mentioned, it just doesn't have an off switch. \n\nEven when I'm doing my job, pushing the buffer or taking out the trash, I feel it. It's just that when I'm moving, the humming kind of blends in with the work, you know? It's like I'm giving the engine somewhere to go. But the second I stop—whether it's sitting in a chair, or waiting in line at the grocery store, or lying in bed—that's when the humming gets so loud, so to speak, that I can't ignore it. It's just always there, humming away, waiting for me to stop moving so it can take over again. It's exhausting, honestly. I just want to be able to sit in a chair or lay in bed and just... be still. But I can't.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"
  }

Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli. Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings). I guess... it doesn't make it go away, if that's what you mean. Like, it's not like a switch flips and the engine turns off just because I stood up or started walking. The humming is still there. But... it's more like, when I'm moving, I'm doing what the humming wants me to do, you know? \n\nLike, if I'm at the church potluck meeting, and I finally just give up and stand up to go pretend to check the thermostat like I told you... the physical act of walking across the fellowship hall feels like a relief, kind of. Because I'm not fighting the urge anymore. When I'm sitting there, my whole brain is just screaming \"move, move, move,\" and it takes so much energy just to keep my rear end in the chair. So when I finally get up, that pressure kind of releases. \n\nBut the humming itself? The actual feeling under my skin? No, that doesn't really stop. It just gets put to use, I guess. Like when I'm buffing the floors at the corporate park. I'm moving, I'm sweating, I'm wrestling with that machine, and the humming is just part of the momentum. It doesn't feel like a relief when I'm working, it just feels like... like that's my normal state. But the minute I stop buffing and lean against the wall to catch my breath? Boom. There it is again, loud and clear, making me want to start the machine right back up. So, moving doesn't really cure it or make it better, it just... it just makes it so I don't feel like I'm going to explode out of my own skin from trying to hold it all in. I don't know if that makes sense, but it's just... it never really leaves me alone, whether I'm moving or not.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nWell, no, not specifically in my legs. I know I mentioned kicking the blankets off and it feeling like my legs want to keep walking, but it's not like... it's not an ache or a cramp or some weird tingling just in my calves or anything like that. I've had guys I work with talk about that, you know, getting charley horses or this creepy-crawly feeling in their shins after being on their feet all day, especially the older guys who've been doing the heavy lifting and floor stripping for twenty-plus years. \n\nBut for me, it's not like it's isolated to my legs. It's more of a whole-body thing. It's that engine feeling I was talking about. It's in my chest, it's in my arms, it's in my head... it's just this overall restlessness. Like my entire body is just humming with this excess energy that doesn't know where to go. So, sure, my legs feel it, and they're usually the first things to start moving when I'm trying to sit still, like bouncing my knee or tapping my foot, but it's not a localized discomfort in my legs. It's just... I'm restless all over. My wife will tell you, it's not just my legs moving in bed, I'm shifting my shoulders, adjusting my arms, rolling my whole torso over. It's just my whole self that can't settle down.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.
